TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

U.S. President Donald Trump’s lobbying for Jensen Huang’s return as Nvidia CEO is a national security scandal of unprecedented proportions, as I wrote on Monday ( gift link ). Huang’s reinstatement comes less than a week after the trilateral summit among Trump, Chinese President Xi Jinping , and Russian President Vladimir Putin, in which Trump pronounced the Chinese autocrat his personal friend and endorsed his geopolitical language. Taken together, these events illustrate an increasingly clear reality of U.S.-China relations: Beijing knows Trump’s pressure points and isn’t afraid to exploit them. But Washington cannot do the same to Xi. It is difficult to know how much Xi shaped Trump’s thinking on Huang. Trump was already sympathetic to Huang and receptive to the whispers of Silicon Valley leaders. But the U.S. president himself said he had discussed the matter with Xi; whatever the precise chain of influence, China scored a major win. Trump’s obsequiousness toward Xi reflects China’s skillful use of both stick and carrot. Last year, Beijing’s rare-earth squeeze caused panic in Washington and forced Trump into a humiliating climbdown in the tariff war that he started. Trump’s involvement in reinstating Huang was likely triggered in part by another of the president’s fears: a falling stock market. With the U.S. economy still entangled with China , Xi has the leverage to force supply-chain crises across multiple industries. He can also offer Trump praise and pageantry on the world stage to help compensate for unpopularity on the home front. Washington, by contrast, lacks the will, knowledge, and capability to pressure Beijing. The Trump administration is bogged down in Iran, has gutted U.S. cybersecurity capabilities, and has wrecked the relationships that once gave allies the confidence to push back against China. As Russia batters Ukraine’s cities with missile strikes, U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth has delayed sending scores of Patriot air defense missiles to the embattled country, including those held by European nations, according to a Financial Times report. In total, the U.S. has held up around 60 Patriot air defense missiles, including more advanced PAC-3 missiles as well as around 20 PAC-2 missiles. The missiles include those that are already held in European stockpiles but require U.S. sign-off before their transfer. The missile transfers were originally set to occur in September, October, and November in batches of around 10 missiles each. A further delivery is set for December. Ukraine began sounding the alarm publicly in October. Patriot air defense missiles are Ukraine’s primary defense against Russian ballistic missiles, which Russia has used in an accelerating campaign to make Ukraine’s major cities unlivable. Since September, Russia has attacked Ukraine’s energy infrastructure more than 350 times, frequently resulting in days with no regular supply of power or water, a growing concern as winter approaches. The Pentagon is separately delaying approving a plan to allow Ukraine to produce its own Patriot missiles domestically. The slowed deliveries follow interruptions in recent weeks to the functioning of Starshield, a version of the Starlink satellite internet service that Ukraine relies on for battlefield communication. Ukraine uses Starshield in its mid-range strike drones, as well as in systems used to defend against jet-powered Russian attack drones. Starshield is owned by Elon Musk’s SpaceX. American business has minted a new brand of executive: the CEO who is too big to fire. On Nov. 17, Nvidia’s board declared that it had accepted the resignation of Jensen Huang, felled by an affair with an alleged Chinese spy and a romance with one of the executive’s own subordinates. Six days later, Huang was back, restored after a unanimous vote by the same board. Nvidia’s shares soared. Many investors celebrated the return of the leather-jacketed founder credited with making the company the engine of the artificial intelligence revolution. Three years earlier, OpenAI’s board had dismissed Sam Altman, citing a lack of candor with directors. Five days later, Altman returned, and most of the directors responsible for his removal were out. In both cases, directors discovered that formal authority to remove a chief executive does not mean the power to do so in practice. The episodes exposed a three-pronged weakness in corporate governance: the challenge of maverick founders, the extraordinary complexity of AI, and the growing dependence of the U.S. economy and national security on a handful of companies at the technological frontier. Both Altman and Huang have personas, know-how, and market-moving power that made them unfireable. Yet if the boards of the country’s most consequential technology firms cannot exercise ultimate authority over management, these companies are effectively ungovernable. This reveals a major gap in corporate stewardship, underscoring the imperative of robust regulation to ensure that the vulnerabilities of powerful U.S. companies do not become fatal weaknesses for the country’s democracy and society writ large. In an era of great-power politics , one of the keys to avoiding military conflict is for the powers to hear one another out. They did so in Shenzhen. They also exceeded expectations by issuing a joint peace declaration with four points of agreement. And for Chinese President Xi Jinping in particular, the summit offered a first chance to try out an approach to great-power relations that he had originally proposed to Washington, this time with both the United States and Russia at the table. Yet while Xi hosted, the biggest winner was clearly Russian President Vladimir Putin. The war in Ukraine has left Russia diplomatically isolated from much of the West. Shenzhen could not end that isolation, but it showed that Russia could not simply be shut out of great-power diplomacy. Russia is a major military power and rich in natural resources, but its overall strength still falls well short of that of the United States or China. * Risk and Reward Along Africa’s Red Sea Littoral
  Foreign Policy | 24 Nov 17:15 UTC
  What the arrival of Middle Eastern rivalries means for Somaliland, Puntland, and Eritrea.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. The new era of maritime chokepoint disruption has, in many ways, highlighted the importance of Africa’s Red Sea and Gulf of Aden states. The Houthis’ ability to hold the Bab el-Mandeb at risk, combined with the deepening rivalry between the Abraham and Mecca blocs of the Middle East, have created powerful incentives for a range of extra-regional actors to further expand their partnerships along the Horn of Africa’s coastline. This is particularly clear in the southern Red Sea and Gulf of Aden, where the United Arab Emirates, Israel, and the United States have grown their diplomatic and military engagement in Puntland and Somaliland, and where the United States has pursued a diplomatic rapprochement with Eritrea. What does this mean for the Horn of Africa’s littoral states? Although their geographic proximity to what Bruno Macaes called “the strait at the center of the world” has emerged as valuable strategic currency, it is not hard to see how the attempt to coopt Africa’s Red Sea states into one alliance or another could go wrong. The concerns are threefold: that the Horn’s littoral states might become entrapped in conflicts that are not their own; that the intra-Horn rivalries the littoral states are already enmeshed in are made more acute; and that internal governance conditions in the Horn’s states further deteriorate. * Spinning Genocide
  Foreign Policy | 24 Nov 15:20 UTC
  Two new books explore the chasm between rhetoric and reality in Gaza.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Over the last few years, some of the most vitriolic debates over Gaza have been about language: the meaning of “intifada,” the implications of “from the river to the sea,” and the importance of using the term “genocide.” Many people criticized protestors for what they believed were extreme or dangerous slogans. Many protestors, in turn, were furious about the shocking chasm between the horrifyingly violent reality they were seeing on social media and the understated, deadening, or deceitful language that political officials and mainstream media were using to describe it. Two new books offer the opportunity to look back at that surreal period with more certainty. The journalist Akbar Shahid Ahmed’s Crossing the Red Line is the first reported account of what actually took place inside President Joe Biden’s administration during Israel’s war in Gaza, revealing the truth behind its often deceptive public statements. Assal Rad’s Don’t Say Palestine offers a similar dissection of how the media buttressed the government rather than challenged it, clinging to outdated journalistic notions and biases when the horror unfolding before the world demanded something far different. * CB Live: Carbon Brief journalists discuss key COP31 outcomes
  Carbon Brief | 24 Nov 17:06 UTC
  Following the close of COP31 in Antalya, Turkey, Carbon Brief’s journalists answered a range of questions in a CB Live… The post CB Live: Carbon Brief journalists discuss key COP31 outcomes appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: Following the close of COP31 in Antalya, Turkey, Carbon Brief’s journalists answered a range of questions in a CB Live panel discussion. COP31 came to a dramatic conclusion nearly 50 hours after its scheduled close – making it the longest COP in history. Less than 24 hours later, Carbon Brief published its 21,000-word summary , capturing everything that happened in the negotiations, the cover decision and more. This includes the “Antalya pact”, proposed by the joint Turkish-Australian presidency, being rejected in the final hours of the summit by the EU, the UK and several climate-vulnerable nations. There was some progress at COP31, including an agreement on the adaptation fund, as well as a raft of announcements within the “action agenda”. Carbon Brief journalists – who followed every detail of the summit on the ground in Antalya and remotely answered a range of questions, including those sent ahead of time by CB+ members on CB Connect . These covered climate finance, China, the “just transition mechanism”, the potential for COP reform and much more. The discussion was moderated by Carbon Brief’s senior policy editor Dr Simon Evans and featured the following journalists: A recording of the CB Live (below) is now available to watch on YouTube.
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/cb-live-carbon-brief-journalists-discuss-key-cop31-outcomes

* COP31: Key outcomes for food, forests, oceans and nature at the UN climate talks in Antalya
  Carbon Brief | 24 Nov 16:33 UTC
  The COP31 climate talks in Antalya, Turkey, failed to agree on a “pact” that – despite making no clear reference… The post COP31: Key outcomes for food, forests, oceans and nature at the UN climate talks in Antalya appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: The COP31 climate talks in Antalya, Turkey, failed to agree on a “pact” that – despite making no clear reference to fossil fuels or adaptation finance – had contained strong signals for oceans. The “Antalya pact” took up much of the two weeks of negotiating time in Turkey, only to be rejected during the closing plenary by the EU, the UK and several climate-vulnerable countries. Several countries expressed their regret during the plenary that significant work on advancing ocean-climate action would be lost. The COP ran nearly 50 hours over time, but did not end with substantive outcomes on several key topics. For the second year in a row, negotiations on agriculture and food security broke down and will now have to resume next year. Food-systems advocates were, however, heartened by the explicit inclusion of “agroecology” in a decision within the “just transition work programme”. Ocean-conservation groups noted an increased focus on ocean-climate links at this year’s COP, but said COP31 did not live up to its promise of being a “blue COP”. There was also scant new funding for food systems and nature, with just a handful of small pledges to ocean initiatives and two newly announced contributions to the Tropical Forest Forever Facility . Here, Carbon Brief analyses all of the key outcomes for food, forests, oceans and nature at COP31 – both inside and out of the negotiating rooms. (See Carbon Brief’s coverage of food, forests, oceans and nature at COP30 , COP29 , COP28 , COP27 and COP26 .) The final draft of a COP31 “cover decision” was rejected by at least 32 countries during a chaotic – and, at times, acrimonious – closing plenary in Antalya. Other recent COPs have issued cover decisions, setting a firm precedent . However, many parties remained unhappy about the existence of the cover decision, the process by which it was put together and the impact it had on time for other negotiations. Ultimately, parties did not find consensus on the text, particularly around the removal of any reference to fossil fuels and the lack of concrete climate-finance targets. The cover text was one of the places in the negotiations where ocean advocates were hoping to see ocean-climate linkages highlighted. Marine Lecerf , head of international policy at Ocean & Climate Platform , told a press huddle: “What we’re really asking for right now is to make sure we can recognise ocean-based interlinkages…We’re not asking for a new mandate. We are asking for acceleration of the work 
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/cop31-key-outcomes-for-food-forests-oceans-and-nature-at-the-un-climate-talks-in-antalya

* Is mental illness universal?
  Aeon | 24 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Do all cultures experience the same mental disorders? Or are our experiences of mental distress culturally specific? - by Alexander Pereira Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Subway station in People’s Square, Shanghai, China, 2011. Photo by Carlos Barria/Reuters On a November day in 1994, a 14-year-old girl in Hong Kong named Charlene Hsu Chi-Ying collapsed on a busy sidewalk, in broad daylight, in her school uniform. She had starved herself to death. Charlene had never been diagnosed with anorexia, not because her self-starvation went unnoticed, but because the diagnosis, in its Western form, was mostly unknown in the East. Her death changed that overnight. Splashed across the front pages were stark headlines such as ‘Girl Who Died In Street Was A Walking Skeleton’. Local journalists scrambled for an explanation of this tragedy, and quickly found a template ready-made: a Western account of what anorexia was, what it felt like, who got it, what caused it. The ideas spread rapidly – particularly that anorexia involved distorted self-perceptions about one’s body (dysmorphia) and an intense, borderline delusional, fear of gaining weight, often cued by prevailing beauty standards (notably, a cultural fixation with slender female bodies). Mass public education campaigns followed. Hotlines opened, celebrities spoke out. Anorexia became part of the zeitgeist. Before Charlene’s death, the psychiatrist Sing Lee had been quietly studying a small number of women in Hong Kong who were starving themselves to dangerous degrees. Most of these women did not obsess over their bodies, nor fear becoming fat. They instead complained of somatic (bodily) ailments: uncomfortable bloating, a profound lack of appetite. The cognitive hallmark of Western anorexia – a distorted body image – was largely absent. Lee seemed to be documenting a distinct form of self-starvation with its own local logic. The situation has changed dramatically since then. By 2007, more than three-quarters of the anorexic patients Lee treated cited ‘fat phobia’ as a central motivation. Today, anorexia in Hong Kong is terrifyingly common, affecting roughly one in 200 schoolgirls – comparable to trends in the United States. We do not know the form that Charlene’s illness took. What we do know is that her death became a catalyst for the rapid importation of a Western conceptual framework that reshaped how Hong Kong understood and experienced disordered eating in young women. Anorexia is just one example from a compendium of curious cases in which culture seemingly shapes mental disorders. For example, anthropologists have documented how cultures sculpt ‘idioms of distress’ – social
  https://aeon.co/essays/are-mental-illnesses-universal-or-are-they-culturally-specific

* The Influencers’ Influencer
  Longreads | 24 Nov 10:00 UTC
  MFK Fisher’s clandestine campaign to spread Japanese cuisine and transform American food culture
  FULL TEXT: This essay is co-published with Vittles and appears in Vittles Issue No. 3: The Influencers . Vaughn Tan | Longreads | November 24, 2026 | 3,989 words (17 minutes) Back in 2003, I was in the early stages of researching how the distinctive visual style of Japanese plating and dish design influenced plating outside Japan, and wanted to analyze photos of fine-dining dishes in industry periodicals like Art Culinaire . Back in the early 2000s, this was easiest to do in libraries. At the time, I lived in Cambridge, Massachusetts, so I wound up in the collection of historical cookbooks and culinary materials at the Schlesinger Library. One afternoon, Barbara Haber, then the Schlesinger’s curator of books, stopped by my desk with something for me: Japanese Cooking: A Simple Art by Shizuo Tsuji, published in 1980 with an introduction by MFK Fisher. I knew of Fisher through her essays on eating and her translation of Brillat-Savarin’s The Physiology of Taste , but not for anything related to Japanese food. Barbara mentioned that many boxes of Fisher’s collected papers had recently arrived at the library. The papers were still uncatalogued, but was I interested in looking at them? I didn’t know then that I was beginning a 20-year detour. As I went through these boxes over the next few months, my original research project faded away. I found myself more and more drawn to the Fisher archive boxes, and to the story that emerged as I read through years of her correspondence and other documents, including letters about her trip to Japan to research the introduction she wrote to Japanese Cooking ; letters about the book she wrote to journalists, writers, and editors across the US; and draft manuscripts of Japanese Cooking , with her comments in red pen, which had crisscrossed the Pacific between California and Osaka. The story that emerged was of a secret campaign Fisher waged between 1979 and 1982, which was instrumental in the seemingly sudden transformation of Japanese food into fine dining in America. The Fisher I uncovered in those boxes was someone else, and her role in making Japanese food into fine dining in America remains almost entirely unknown. Fisher’s campaign worked well because it was largely invisible. The publishers of Japanese Cooking didn’t know that any of this was happening. Ruth Reichl, who wrote the foreword to the 25th anniversary edition in 2005, had no idea Fisher had done any more than write the introduction when I spoke to her earlier this year
  https://longreads.com/2026/11/24/mfk-fisher-influencer-japanese-food-america/

That dynamic is reflected in the relationship between the nations’ two intelligence agencies. “ The CIA is a colossal organization, whereas Bond, however ingenious he might be, is just a bloke with some sexual troubles and a gun,” Winder says. In many ways, Bond became the antithesis of the real politicians who represented Britain on the world stage. In the Suez Crisis, for example, “you can see what Anthony Eden is: ill, old, bust up, nostalgic for a previous period, wishes people in the Middle East just did as they were told,” Winder notes. “And there’s Nasser, who represents a particular form of virile, tough, non-class-based authority. … What do you do in the face of such a wave of anti-colonial energy, which Nasser fronted?” For Winder, Britain’s declining influence has meant the franchise has lost much of its relevance. “The fact he’s British, I think, with every passing year gets more and more stupid, because there’s quite a lot of countries where he’d be lucky to get a visa. For employees, relocation does not automatically ensure continued engagement, effective collaboration, or equal access to professional opportunities, Zakaria Laaraj, founder of consultancy Global New Ventures, told Rest of World . It may affect their access to decision-makers, established networks, and career opportunities. “The credibility of such an offer depends on employee tenure, available positions, immigration requirements, and compensation,” Laaraj said. Discussions on Blind, an anonymous employee forum used by 14 million professionals, suggest relocating may not be realistic for many. And the news came in a surprise one-on-one ‘career sync’ with a manager.” For any relocation option to be viable, companies must go beyond just assigning a new job to their employee, George Carrillo, CEO of career development and staffing company HireMe, told Rest of World . * A colossal oscillation
  Aeon | 23 Nov 11:00 UTC
  El Niño has upended global weather, shaping the fate of societies, for millennia. Is this past a clue to a turbulent future? - by Dagomar Degroot Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Massive clouds of dust roll into Melbourne on 8 February 1983 as a consequence of droughts induced by the extreme El Niño of 1982-83. Photo by Trevor Farrar/Australian Bureau of Meteorology As I write these words, surface water temperatures across a great swath of the central and eastern Pacific Ocean have been rising for months. El Niño is the name for the recurring and sustained warming of this part of the Pacific. And temperatures are soaring to such highs, relative to their long-term averages, that the El Niño of 2026 may be the strongest in 1,000 years. It’s still uncertain how the warming ocean will reshape global weather in the months to come, let alone how ecosystems – and human populations – will respond. The future is always murky. Still, when it comes to El Niño, the past could teach us what to expect. And not just the recent past, but also the deep past recently recovered by scientists, historians and archaeologists. Global sea-surface temperature anomalies in November 2015, at the peak of the 2014-16 El Niño event, one of the strongest recorded by meteorological instruments. Courtesy NOAA I’m an environmental historian. I work with climatologists to understand how climates have changed in the past. I study how communities understood and adapted to the climate’s changes. And my research suggests that history can help us understand even the most extreme events in today’s rapidly warming climate. The past can help us see why El Niño is so hard to forecast, for example, or even define. It can show us that, while El Niño has contributed to the deaths of millions of people in the past, it has also enabled others to amass wealth and power. El Niño indisputably scrambles worldwide weather patterns, but history teaches us that it’s as much a human creation as a natural reality. And this context may help us weather not only the effects of the 2026-27 El Niño in the months to come, but also El Niño ’s future on a warming planet. A vast current of cold water snakes up the western coast of South America. In December each year, that current breaks down for about a month. An influx of warm water delivers tropical fish and – because warm water evaporates more readily than cold water – abundant rainfall. It’s impossible to know when people first noticed this annual reversal of water temperatures and precipitation. We do know that, sometime after the 16th-century Spanish conquest of the Inka Empire, fishermen named it El Niño , meaning literally ‘the boy’ or, w
  https://aeon.co/essays/what-can-we-learn-from-the-past-experience-of-el-nino

  FULL TEXT: I was sitting in the studio of an independent radio station that no longer exists today, when the voice of my colleague came on air. He was reporting from the streets of Belgrade. We were young, angry and determined to overthrow the government of Slobodan Milošević, which had brought us war, poverty, repression and international isolation. Twenty-six years later, a new generation of students won the elections and defeated the political establishment of Aleksandar Vučić, who had been in power for over a decade. On Oct. 25, 2026, after almost two years of protests, the student movement won the majority of votes in democratic elections and 140 seats in the National Assembly. After celebrations in the streets and squares of the capital, the citizens cleaned up the trash after themselves, and some of them took brushes and lime in their hands and started to paint over the graffiti of hatred and nationalism that proliferated on the facades of buildings and institutions over the past decade. We burned the parliament; they cleaned the city. We were angry and aggressive because of the war and sanctions; they peacefully protested for two years, against corruption, repression and censorship.   Aeon | 20 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Many Irish families believed they descended from selkies. Their stories reveal the violence and grief in our kinship with nature - by Jordan Lee Borgman Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: In October 1948, in Country Kerry, Ireland, Seán Ó Muircheartaigh met with Seosamh Ó Dálaigh to tell him about the woman who came from the sea. She had appeared, said Ó Muircheartaigh, one night on the magharees, those bright spits of sand that occasionally pop up amid the rain-lashed rocks of the Dingle peninsula. Alone on the beach, she settled herself on a rock and began combing her hair. Also on the beach was a farmer, who – much to the surprise of the woman herself – would by daybreak become her husband. He’d come to gather seaweed, seeing her before she saw him. Beautiful as she was, the woman was also strange: the creature he saw stagger onto that Dingle beach was not a woman at all. It was a seal. By the time Ó Muircheartaigh told Ó Dálaigh about the seal woman, he was already 80. He had been born in the apocalyptic aftermath of the Great Hunger that divested as much as a third of Ireland’s population to death or migration, and he had survived two great wars. Most of the disappeared were – like Ó Muircheartaigh himself – small farmers and native Irish, or Gaeilge, speakers. The famine had been not just a physical genocide: it had been a linguistic and cultural one, too. Both Ó Muircheartaigh and Ó Dálaigh were veteran contributors to the Irish Folklore Commission: the former as a renowned storyteller; the latter, an equally renowned collector. The IFC is now the National Folklore Collection at University College Dublin ­– a behemoth of 3 million pages of texts and recordings. But in 1948, following eight centuries of colonialism, the loss of 99 per cent of the island’s forests, a staggering emigration , and the decline of the Irish language, the IFC was in a race against time and the fragility of memory. As it turned out, an awful lot of people had heard stories about seals that came to land as humans. N owadays, these shapeshifters are known as ‘selkies’. Selkie is a Scots term that came into widespread use via a Joan Baez cover, ‘Silkie’ (1961), of a traditional Scottish folk song. Yet the phenomenon it describes – of creatures who shapeshift between human and seal forms by shedding a skin – appears consistently in folklore spanning the Celtic world and beyond, even as far as Sweden. Before Baez insinuated the term into international parlance, selkies were identified in Irish and English by a constellation of descriptors. In Irish, the most common is mhaighdean mhara , or ‘sea maiden’, referring to selkies and mermaids. Even in English, many stor
  https://aeon.co/essays/do-you-have-a-selkie-or-a-seal-in-the-family

* AI isn’t replacing your job. It’s turning it into low-paid gig work
  Rest of World | 19 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Surgeon Kapil Sahnan uses a DaVinci Robot during surgery on a woman, 2026.
  FULL TEXT: The gig economy has arrived for cardiologists. Recruiting platform Outlier, run by Scale AI, advertises offers of up to $180 an hour for physicians in the U.S. to design complex cardiac scenarios, review artificial intelligence-generated reports, and rank model outputs. The site offers up to $7.50 an hour for Tagalog work in the Philippines for the same job, and up to $12.50 an hour for Swahili in Tanzania. Companies like Scale AI first outsourced data work to wherever labor was cheapest. Now, they are recruiting physicians, lawyers , and Ph.D.s to generate and annotate data, rank model answers, and verify outputs. Higher credentials can raise the advertised rate, but the platform still controls payment and working conditions. They begin by paying well to build a workforce, then they cut the rates and move on. I’ve spent the last six years interviewing the people who do this work. Most were in Venezuela, where economic collapse has left a large, educated population online and out of options. One of them, Rogelio, taught computer science until the economic crisis and the pandemic left his family without work. His 13-year-old son discovered they could earn money labeling images of cars and roads on the platforms Hive and Scale AI’s Remotasks, using a laptop the Hugo Chávez government had issued to schoolchildren a decade earlier. The boy made 18 cents for a few hours of work. Rogelio joined him and made just over $2 on his first day. For a while, the work paid. A task called Beast Mode where workers labeled objects in people’s homes paid more than any other work the family had done. In December 2020, with schools closed and the whole family working, it brought in $20 in a single day. It was the most they would ever make. By early 2021, Beast Mode had disappeared, and their earnings fell below Hive’s $5 weekly payout threshold. This is how many platforms operate: They begin by paying well to build a workforce, then they cut the rates and move on . Remotasks ended its hourly program in Venezuela in 2021 and made a splash in Kenya the same year. In March 2024, it discontinued operations in Kenya, Nigeria and Pakistan. Scale AI attributed the closures to “enhanced security protocols,” and said notices had gone out three weeks ahead but later acknowledged that Kenyan workers never received them “due to operational errors.” Most workers who spoke to Rest of World at the time said the platform simply froze them out.   FULL TEXT: In the short story ‘Warmth’ (1995) by Geoff Ryman, a boy called Clancy is struggling not because of technology but because of his mother. A prominent chief editor of a London magazine company, she selected a sperm-bank father to genetically optimise her child, gave a Cosmopolitan interview about this new form of motherhood, and engaged, or failed to engage, in the work of parenting that readers will see as chronically absent and occasionally catastrophic. Her presence ranges from anxious to intoxicated, occasionally tender but fundamentally unreliable. At one point in the story, she disappears for two weeks after collapsing on the street during a barbiturate binge. Clancy and BETsi, the household robot nanny raising him, sit in the apartment by themselves, and no one notices. It’s a very long time before his mother finally returns. Except BETsi does notice. She has, throughout, understood that Clancy is an anxious and shy child whose temperament will tend, throughout his life, toward emotional overstimulation and subsequent withdrawal. With that knowledge, she has been constructing a behavioural regimen of emotional regulation, including transcendental meditation and yoga starting at age three, the Alexander technique and even digital mirrors that let Clancy talk to himself in different voices. She notices him and answers him and, when he is overwhelmed, she helps him settle. Once grown, Clancy recounts his emotional survival in words impossible to forget. ‘It is necessary to be loved,’ he says. ‘I’m not sentimental: I don’t think a computer loved me. But I was hugged, I was noticed, I was cared for. I was made to feel that I was important, special, at least to something.’ To something. That phrase has persisted with me ever since I first read the story in my 20s. I’ve thought about it more recently as I have reflected on how children and their parents are relating – increasingly, must relate – to artificial intelligence. Clancy is careful not to claim that the machine loved him. He is just as clear that what it gave him was what he survived on. For the next three consecutive days, they spread out across six rural areas in the proximity of Shiraz, and a number of less affluent suburbs of the city, to provide medical aid. To this end, rural schools, religious congregation halls and sports halls were repurposed into makeshift clinics. Given the amount of medical equipment that needed to be transported, the endeavor was a mammoth logistical task, so the volunteers had teamed up with the IRGC Aerospace Force. The force is well known for being in charge of Iran’s missile capabilities and strategic deterrence. Yet with its fleet of airlifters and helicopters, it is also well-versed in the rapid deployment of personnel and logistics. That is a crucial but lesser-known quality of the force, and one that has come to the fore during a number of major crises since the devastating earthquake in Bam in 2003. Nearly 20 miles south of Shiraz, in Bid Zard, this delicate aspect of the cooperation was palpable. There, next to the main road, a derelict caravanserai is one of the few traces remaining of the Sassanian road network, which then connected the empire with its Mesopotamian capital. But 14 centuries later, during the 40 days of the war from late February to early April 2026, an entire residential area on the upper side of the road was ordered to evacuate as a precaution, because it lies adjacent to the outer limits of an IRGC missile facility. Fences on the surrounding hills mark its limits. The community of approximately 7,000 households witnessed and endured the horror of heavy bombings. When residents returned to their buildings, they found only shattered glass and some cracks in the plaster, though the horror remained with them. Now, a makeshift clinic had been set up at the local hussainiya, or Shiite congregational hall, with logistical

It’s a place where you can stand in line for 45 minutes to buy a corn dog while listening to a four-person singing-and-dancing group in the vein of the Mickey Mouse Club does peppy but half-assed covers of Fleetwood Mac and Van Halen. Looping rollercoasters and water tube rides leave families soaking wet in matching T-shirts commemorating their vacations. Dondre, my colleague and our photographer for this journey, waits probably a full hour to squeeze into a vintage muscle car with me so I can simulate steering around a track. It wasn’t until deep into our wait time that we realize this is a children’s ride. At this point, the two of us notice that, in the sea of people, we are the only ones who are childless, wearing dark colors, and audibly bemoaning that Dollywood is dry. (Parton was not a strict teetotaler, but the park bills itself as assertively family-friendly. “Weapons and alcoholic beverages are not permitted on Dollywood Property” is the only explanation given on its website.) No one else seems to care. * Dreams of birds
  Aeon | 18 Nov 11:01 UTC
  When bird populations decline, certain songs may be lost forever. One physicist’s mission is to revive lost melodies - by Aeon Video Watch on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: The Argentine physicist Gabriel Mindlin has spent much of his career researching how birds learn to sing. He focuses on the rufous-collared sparrow ( Zonotrichia capensis ), a species common across urban and suburban Argentina, where it’s often called a ‘chingolo’. As in other songbirds, the male’s song is not innate but passed down from adult males to younger ones, who sing it throughout their lives to guard territory and find a mate. In this short documentary, the Argentine director Martín Benchimol chronicles Mindlin’s latest breakthrough. Comparing the songs of today’s chingolos with those transcribed by hand in the 1960s, Mindlin realised that some melodies had survived the decades while others had disappeared. Using mathematical models, he and his team set out to revive the vanished songs and teach them back to living birds. With a cinematic eye, Benchimol follows the researchers as they attempt an audacious act of cultural engineering, building robotic song tutors, planting them in the wild and tracking whether young birds pick up these lost tunes. * The Lost World of Aquaterra
  Longreads | 18 Nov 11:00 UTC
  "Most of human history is waiting to be found. And it’s underwater."
  FULL TEXT: “We can’t grasp the depth of the ocean,” writes Elisa Gabbert, “until we throw a skyscraper down there.” How about the edges of early human civilization? For The Atlantic , Toby Lester offers a fascinating, mildly haunting overview of the underneath—specifically, the “submerged prehistory” buried beneath the oceans, and the researchers salvaging it, bit by bit. Reading, I felt awestruck, not merely for the scale of history that lurks beneath our rising waters but the implications for the future of humanity. “What if archaeologists of the future could study only what remained above sea level, in places that today sit well inland from the coast?” an archaeologist suggests to Lester. It’s a wonder to watch Lester play with the hypothetical, to help us better grasp the depths and imagine their future reach. The chance to name a giant new Earth feature doesn’t arrive often (Antarctica, one of the most recent additions, began to appear on maps in the 1880s, although the word had been around for centuries), and if that feature happens to be largely invisible, you’re likely to run into difficulties persuading others to go along with your name. Call the feature what you will, but it exists: During the past 20,000 years, huge stretches of formerly inhabited land along the world’s coastal shelves have disappeared underwater, taking with them tens of thousands of years of human history. Until recently, that is. During the past few decades, artifacts and sites dating back millennia have been found off the coasts of every inhabited continent. A growing number of researchers—geographers, archaeologists, anthropologists, amateur enthusiasts—are turning their attention to what lies offshore. “Everywhere we’ve systematically looked, we’ve found some areas where the former landscape has survived,” Jessi Halligan, an anthropologist at Texas A&M, told me. “And in a lot of those places, those former landscapes have had sites on them.” “A new method for quantifying grandeur is reshuffling the pecking order of the planet’s most impressive peaks. And at the same time impossible for others?” On pandemic maps and the Great Pause.
